No causal policy succeeds in both worlds, and with equal priors none succeeds more than half the time | PO-SENS-07 (identical transcripts and disjoint success sets exclude every causal policy; bound (1 + TV)/2); `FROB-REF-445`; GATE-SENS-09 | §20.12 |
| CX-207 | A sequential sensing rule that reaches 90% accuracy keeps it under a deadline | Reads right with probability 3/4; a rule that stops when one class leads by two reads is right 90% of the time with 3.2 reads on average, but is still undecided after six reads with probability 27/512, and forcing a guess then yields 225/256, below 90% | Every sensing policy is compared under the mission's deadline, with delay priced (§20.12) | §20.12 |
| CX-208 | The right sensor exists, so the mission is solvable whatever the packet timing | Docking fixture with the reading of v₁ arriving after the third command is committed: a causal controller issues one command vector in both worlds, and the final position g·(2p + u₁) cannot equal 1 for both g = 1 and g = 2. All 561 grid cells are excluded | An alias witness whose cause is timing; the seven instants and the delivery deadline are part of the verdict (§20.16, ADR-SENS-03); GATE-SENS-09, GATE-SENS-13 | §20.16, §20.12 |
| CX-209 | A policy may be made equivariant under the whole mechanical symmetry group 𝔾 | A quadruped symmetric fore-aft and left-right has 𝔾 = C₂ × C₂ of order four. With a forward depth camera on the sagittal plane the fore-aft reflection and the half-turn are not symmetries of the closed loop: an obstacle ahead is seen, one behind is not. 𝔾_obs has order two; augmentation doubles the data, it does not quadruple it | INV-SENS-08 and ADR-SENS-11: closed-loop consumers use 𝔾_obs only; `FROB-REF-443`; GATE-SENS-10 | §20.13 |
| CX-210 | A sensor mounted on the plane of symmetry preserves the reflection | A spinning lidar on the plane of symmetry: a reflection reverses the order in which beams fire, so with per-beam instants the mirrored robot would need a lidar that spins the other way; the reflection is broken at first order in the product of body rate and scan time. An IMU mounted off the plane breaks the reflection of raw observations too | 𝔾_obs preserves each sensor's pattern including its timing (§20.13); the report states whether the scan was treated as instantaneous | §20.13 |
| CX-211 | An inconsistent loop of pairwise calibrations identifies the bad measurement | Scalar offsets, unit weights, one edge biased by β. Three sensors with three pairwise edges: the least-squares residual is β/3 on every edge, so the loop is known to be inconsistent and the bad edge is not identified. Four sensors with six edges: β/2 on the bad edge, β/4 on the four edges sharing a node with it, zero on the opposite edge | Fault localization needs redundancy beyond one cycle; on a tree r_⊥ is identically zero and localization is refused (`FROB-REF-446`); PO-SENS-08; GATE-SENS-11 | §20.14 |
| CX-212 | The consistency residual can be taken with the ordinary Euclidean projector | Rotations in radians and translations in metres have different scales; the projector I − B·(Bᵀ·W·B)⁻¹·Bᵀ·W is orthogonal in the metric W, and the Euclidean projector gives a different and wrong decomposition whenever W is not a multiple of the identity (no numeric case is given) | PO-SENS-08; the negative control of GATE-SENS-11 (anisotropic weights) | §20.14 |
| CX-213 | A boundary may be tested with a non-strict comparison | Docking at U = ¾ with the probe p = 5/8 and ε = 5/16 exactly: both worlds can report 15/16 while requiring u₁ = −¼ (g = 1) and u₁ = −¾ (g = 2). The boundary is ε < (U + ½)/4 with equality excluded; nine of the 561 grid cells lie exactly on it | PO-SENS-06; strict predicates in exact arithmetic (class D0, §35); GATE-SENS-13 | §20.16 |
| CX-214 | A verdict survives a small widening of the hypothesis set | Admit a third gain 33/32 beside {1, 2}. At U = ¾ and ε = 1/64 the reading (65/64)·p is possible for g = 1 and for g = 33/32 under every first command; both worlds receive the same remaining commands; final positions are c and 33c/32 and the smallest achievable worst error is 1/65 m at c = 64/65. Every cell admitted under the two-gain mission is wrong for the three-gain mission | A verdict is bound to the declared support of the hidden parameters, the delivery deadline, the error units and the target tolerance; a change to any of them invalidates it (§20.16); GATE-SENS-13 | §20.16 ≡ CX-049 |
| CX-215 | A noise generator may hand out draws in call order | Adding, removing or reordering a sensor that no controller consumes shifts the draws of every other sensor, so the first added sensor changes what the others read (no numeric case is given here; §35.9 gives the r₀, r₁, r₂ case) | ADR-SENS-10: noise streams keyed by world address, sensor, sample index and component; INV-SENS-06; GATE-SENS-03 | §20.17, §20.2 |

### N.18 §21 Spark: GPU backends and the kernel toolchain

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-216 | A strict inequality can be decided by a bare `f32` comparison | Is 16,777,216 less than 16,777,217? In integers, yes. In `f32` the second number (2²⁴ + 1) is not representable and rounds to the first, so the strict comparison is false. In the recorded pilot on a 65,536-row grid dense near a boundary, the bare `f32` sign gave 452 false inclusions and 23,438 false exclusions (36%), identically on every device; the exact integer kernel gave none | ADR-SPARK-03: exact-integer and guarded kernels for strict predicates and digests; `FROB-REF-252`; PO-SPARK-02; GATE-SPARK-08 | §21.8, §35.7, §20.16 |
| CX-217 | A kernel belongs on the GPU because it is a kernel | The same boundary pilot end to end: the host integer loop took about 34 µs against about 290 µs on Metal (of which under 13 µs was device time) and over 1.2 ms on one RTX 4090; two-card sharding was slower than one card | Representation before placement; a small host-resident job stays on the host; the planner's cost model (§21.8 rules 1 and 3, §21.12 rule 4) | §21.8, §21.12 |
| CX-218 | Strides can be copied verbatim between array libraries | A 2 × 2 contiguous `f32` view has strides (8, 4) in signed bytes and (2, 1) in unsigned elements. Copying (8, 4) as element strides onto a 16-element buffer reads element 8 + 4 = 12 at index [1, 1], where element 3 belongs; both reads are in bounds, so nothing faults | Views carry stride unit and signedness; `FROB-REF-617`; admission tests INT-FNP-01 and INT-FT-01 | §21.5 |
| CX-219 | A mask can be applied as an arithmetic blend m·a + (1 − m)·b | An infinity in the unselected arm becomes a NaN, since 0·∞ is NaN | Dialect rule 8: a mask is a select; §35.5 condition 4 | §21.6, §35.7 |
| CX-220 | A double-float accumulator works on any compiler | The error-free sum of 1 and 2⁻³⁰ must return (1, 2⁻³⁰); a compiler that simplifies the error term algebraically returns (1, 0), and the accumulator silently loses its low word. The product canary: (1 + 2⁻¹²)² must return (1 + 2⁻¹¹, 2⁻²⁴) | ADR-SPARK-10: split product, safe math mode, start-up canaries; `FROB-REF-609` | §21.14 |
| CX-221 | A persistent launch with a grid-wide barrier is a safe way to step a whole stretch | In the suite's piano renderer such a barrier on Metal "very rarely" leaves one thread group unscheduled, "about once per few hundred launches", and the host reruns the stretch | Dialect rule 6: no grid-wide barrier; a stage that needs every world to finish is two launches (ADR-SPARK-09) | §21.6 |
| CX-222 | Padding a mixed batch to its largest member costs little | Ω_p = L_max^p / E[L^p]: degrees of freedom 18 and 35 mixed evenly cost 1.3, 1.6 and 1.8 for p = 1, 2, 3; mixed 90 to 10 they cost 1.8, 3.0 and 4.5 | Size buckets with capacities 12, 18, 24, 32, 48, 64 (Ω₁ ≤ 1.45, Ω₃ ≤ 3.1 inside a bucket); KILL-BUCKET | §21.11 |
| CX-223 | Adding the second machine's GPU to training gives fleet speedup | The Mac's GPU adds at most its share of the pair of cards: 0.125 at 25% of one RTX 4090, 0.30 at 60%. Gain can come only from moving non-training work off the training cards, at most 1/(1 − g); a factor of 1.5 needs g ≥ 1/3 | The fleet scheduler is funded only if the phase-ledger pilot shows g ≥ 1/3; KILL-FLEET | §21.17 |
| CX-224 | Per-design specialized kernels should be the default path | On an incumbent's event trace of a humanoid, of 262 ns per world-step the stages a specialized recursion could touch sum to 53 ns (20%): an infinitely fast recursion gives 262/209 = 1.25, twice as fast 1.11; a manipulation scene gives 1.19; cold kernel compilation takes about 96 s | ADR-SPARK-08: survivors only, only at a specializable share of at least one third, bit-identical to the generic kernel; KILL-SPECIALIZE | §21.18, §21.20 |
| CX-225 | Factor the contact Hessian in half precision on tensor cores and refine in single precision | Half precision has unit round-off 2⁻¹¹ ≈ 4.9·10⁻⁴ and refinement needs κ·u < 1. On contact states of three robots after unit-diagonal scaling, κ·u ≥ 1 in 37 to 64% of states and refinement fails outright in 9 to 18%. A rank-one worst case has κ = 1 + 48/0.01 = 4,801, so κ·u ≈ 2.3. Each refinement step costs about 3n² against n³/3, a ratio of 9k/n: 2.3 at n = 27 and k = 7 | Single precision in scaled variables (§35.10); half precision is not used anywhere in the step (§21.14) | §21.20 |
| CX-226 | Copy-on-write forks of device world state save copying | State is write-dense: every step rewrites every world's configuration, velocity, contacts and warm starts, so a fork has copied everything after one step and paid for indirection in every read. The whole copy is small: 8,192 worlds at 30 kB are 0.25 GB, about a millisecond | Fork by indexed copy of chosen rows into free slots (`K-snapshot`) | §21.20 |
| CX-227 | Brick directories need minimal perfect hashing or succinct structures | A dense directory with 4-byte slots is 63 × 63 × 19 = 75,411 slots, 0.3 MB, for a 10 × 10 × 3 m interior at 2 cm and 18.8 MB for a 60 × 40 × 8 m warehouse at 2 cm, under 1.5% of the scene it indexes; a hash adds a second, verifying read to every lookup | A dense directory per scene variant over a shared brick pool (§19) | §21.20 |
| CX-228 | A mergeable quantile sketch gives a statistic that does not depend on arrival | A two-centroid sketch that merges its closest pair, fed {0, 3, 5, 9}: in the order 0, 3, 5, 9 it ends as (8/3 with weight 3; 9 with weight 1); in the order 0, 9, 5, 3 it ends as (1.5 with weight 2; 7 with weight 2) | Integer log-bin histograms (`K-x-hist`), merged by addition; a quantile is reported as a bin interval (§35.7) | §21.20 |
| CX-229 | On unified memory the CPU can read live GPU state with zero copy | Shared storage must be quiescent before the other processor reads it; and the data are tiny: a humanoid's integration state is about 540 bytes, so shadowing 256 worlds at 50 Hz moves 6.9 MB/s | Fenced snapshots under rule R1 and epoch leases (`FROB-REF-614`); INV-SPARK-06 | §21.20, §21.10 |
| CX-230 | Unordered floating-point scatter is acceptable if 99.9% of windows replay bitwise | 0.999¹⁰⁰⁰ = 0.37: a campaign of a thousand windows would replay exactly about one time in three | Stable-key sort and segmented reduction; floating-point atomics banned (§35.6, §35.7) | §21.20 |
| CX-231 | Tensor-core products for a 256-wide policy speed training | The learner and inference together are about 13% of training wall-clock on one incumbent stack, so their removal gives at most 1.15; reduced precision also leaves class D2 | Plain `f32` products in the dialect; device matrix paths only inside the labeled D4 profile (§35.2) | §21.20, §36.4 |
| CX-232 | Per-world adaptive time tiles between interactions save work | Separate environments are already independent; policy and sensor frames at 50 to 100 Hz bound a tile to 5 to 20 steps; shapes become dynamic | Fixed decimation in a static launch graph (INV-SPARK-05) | §21.20 |
| CX-233 | Full-physics sampling control can be the default evaluator | At the smallest published setting (30 samples, 3 iterations, 0.9 s horizon, 50 Hz) a simulated second costs 2.0 M world-steps against 500 for a policy, a factor of 4·10³: a battery of 256 twenty-second episodes takes about 86 minutes of a device at 2.0 M world-steps/s against 1.3 s | A sampling controller for single worlds and as a fallback (§22); never the evaluator of record | §21.20 |

### N.19 §22 Reflex: estimation, control, gaits, skills, shields

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-234 | Score an estimator after fitting a scale | An estimate of 2 m for a true motion of 1 m has zero residual after a fitted factor of ½; aligned by one translation and one yaw only, it scores 1 m | Scoring rule of §22.3; FROB-REF-464 | §22.3 |
| CX-235 | Report "no solution" when the cone solver fails to converge | A 50 N body pushed with 30 N at μ = 0.5 is infeasible, and the proof is the dual vector y = (0.5, −1) with yᵀb = −5 N; an unconverged run has no such vector and proves nothing | Three outcomes of §22.6; PO-REFLEX-03 (rational re-check) | §22.5 |
| CX-236 | Take the lower rows of a space-frame screw Jacobian as the tool-point Jacobian | Two-link arm, links 0.4 m and 0.3 m at angles 0 and π/2, limit 2.5 N·m: the point Jacobian gives abs(F_x) ≤ 25/3 N from joint 2; the space-frame rows give a different bound | Typed Jacobians (C-02); GATE-REFLEX-08 | §22.10 |
| CX-237 | Compute gait stability on the symmetric subspace only | ẋ = −x, ẏ = +y with symmetry y ↦ −y: on Fix(K) = {y = 0} the multiplier is e^(−T); the full-space multiplier in the antisymmetric block is e^(+T) > 1. The restricted answer says stable for a robot that falls sideways | PO-GAIT-01 (full monodromy); GATE-GAIT-01 | §22.9.2 |
| CX-238 | Resolve simultaneous impacts in a fixed order | A uniform bar on supports at ±l/3 (β = 1/3) arriving at v: the coupled solve gives rest; one support first leaves the first end at v/4 with 1/16 of the kinetic energy. The sign of (1 − β)/m decides whether order matters | Coupled solve of simultaneous events; no refusal for simultaneity alone (GATE-GAIT-02) | §22.9 |
| CX-239 | Budget the payload's kinetic energy alone | Rotor inertia 5 × 10⁻⁵ kg·m² behind 50:1 on a 0.05 m wheel is 50 kg of effective mass, against an 8 kg cart; T_ub must come from the full mass matrix | `shield.budget`; GATE-SHIELD-01 | §22.11 |
| CX-240 | Project onto the constraint row, then clip to the actuator box | R = I, u_π = (1, 0), box [0, 1]², row u₁ + u₂ ≥ 1.5: the unboxed projection (1.25, 0.25) leaves the box; clipped it is (1, 0.25) and violates the row; the breakpoint solution is u = (1, 0.5) | PO-SHIELD-02; GATE-SHIELD-02 | §22.12 |
| CX-241 | Estimate a monitor's miss rate from the monitor's own log | A monitor silent on every breach it cannot see reports δ₁ = 0 from its own output | Independent hidden-state labels from the simulator (§22.12); GATE-SHIELD-04 | §22.12 |
| CX-242 | A transported skill refines its specification if it can select the good branch | A target with successors {stable, fall} cannot refine an abstract transition whose only successor is stable | FROB-REF-444; GATE-REFLEX-08 | §22.10 |
| CX-243 | Use the fixed-base pullback τ = JᵀF for a floating-base robot | The map must come from the contact-consistent constrained solve (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Limits written into every `.fskill` artifact | §22.10 |
| CX-244 | Tie a controller's weights under the mechanical symmetry group | Two latches: the in-law controller succeeds and the tied deterministic one fails; on a one-sided patch tying under Γ_mech loses return | Stabilizer intersection of §22.13; GATE-REFLEX-07 | §22.13 |
| CX-245 | Use the sampling controller as an acceptance evaluator because one episode is cheap | Bounding a 1% failure rate at 95% takes 299 episodes: about 99 and 299 GPU-minutes under sampling control against about 10 and 60 to train once | Screen, not evaluator (§22.8); the planted acceptance request is refused | §22.8 |

### N.20 §23 Cortex: the gym, learning, family policies, inheritance

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-246 | One `done` flag is enough for bootstrap targets | A true 8 s deadline with terminal reward 0 needs target 0; an external cut on a continuing task with V = 10, γ = 0.99 needs 9.9. One flag gives 0 for both | Termination and truncation are distinct (E-rules of §23.3; P-1); GATE-CORTEX-06 | §23.3 |
| CX-247 | A per-step reward is the same reward at any control rate | A penalty of 1 per control step over 10 s totals 500 at 50 Hz and 1,000 at 100 Hz for the same motion | Time-aware rewards: rates integrated over simulated time; GATE-CORTEX-06 | §23.3 |
| CX-248 | A per-step discount is the same discount at any control rate | With γ = 0.99 per step, a constant reward rate is worth 2.0·r at 20 ms and 1.0·r at 10 ms; with γ(Δt) = exp(−0.5025·Δt) it is 2.000·r and 1.995·r | Physical discounting (P-7) | §23.3 |
| CX-249 | The density of the sampled Gaussian is the likelihood of the applied clipped action | Unit-variance Gaussians clipped at +1 with means 0 and 1: the endpoint-mass ratio is 0.5/0.158655 = 3.15149; the latent densities give e^0.5 = 1.64872 | P-3; a mixture of laws is refused (FROB-REF-527) | §23.6 |
| CX-250 | The clipped surrogate's branch test is the same for both signs of the advantage | Negative advantage, ε = 0.2, ratios 0.5, 1, 2: the correct finite differences are 0, −0.5, −1; the reversed branch gives −0.25, −0.5, 0 | P-4; GATE-CORTEX-07 | §23.6 |
| CX-251 | The value loss may use the value stored at collection | Critic output 2, stored value and return 0: the gradient of c_V·(V − R)² is 4·c_V; the stored-value form gives 0 | P-5; GATE-CORTEX-07 | §23.6 |
| CX-252 | Concatenated episodes may be evaluated as one sequence | Two independently reset one-step episodes: the frozen policy's ratios are 1 and 1; reading across the reset gives 1.582459 | P-6; GATE-CORTEX-07 | §23.6 |
| CX-253 | Average the shard means | One sample with gradient 1 and three with gradient 0, shards of sizes 1 and 3: the global mean is 0.25; the mean of shard means is 0.5 | Normalization rule of §23.9; P-10 | §23.9 |
| CX-254 | Normalize with each shard's own variance | Shards {0, 0} and {4}: global mean 4/3 and variance 32/9; each shard's own variance is 0 | Normalization rule of §23.9; P-10 | §23.9 |
| CX-255 | Copy stride numbers between array libraries | A contiguous 2 × 2 `f32` view has strides (8, 4) in signed bytes and (2, 1) in unsigned elements; copied verbatim onto a 16-element buffer, index [1, 1] reads element 12 where element 3 belongs, in bounds; strides (8, −4) have no unsigned form | One conversion boundary (§23.4); GATE-CORTEX-09; admission test at INT-FNP-01 and INT-FT-01 | §23.4 |
| CX-256 | Take the minimum of raw margins in mixed units | min(0.05 m, 12 °C) is 0.05 in metres and 12 with the clearance in millimetres; with scales 0.10 m and 10 °C the margins are 0.5 and 1.2 and the clearance binds in every unit system | Unit-normalized predicates (C-30); GATE-CORTEX-10 | §23.5 |
| CX-257 | A learning gate passes when the pipeline runs | An exported all-zero policy head passes a plumbing test; zero-weight, random and shuffled-label policies must fail the return threshold | Behaviour, not plumbing (§38.11); GATE-CORTEX-01, -14 | §23.16, §23.21 |
| CX-258 | A ranking found under a pyramidal (box) friction cone is a ranking | A planted box-cone exploit flips a planted ranking; the result is typed `model-conditional` and an honest policy on the circular cone is not flagged | Rank robustness of finalists (§23.16); GATE-CORTEX-15; FROB-REF-830 | §23.16 |

### N.21 §24 Arena: tasks, requirements, fixtures, metrics, qualification

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-259 | Compose raw requirement margins with a minimum | Clearance 0.02 m and thermal margin 3 K: the raw minimum is 0.02 (clearance binds); in millimetres it is 3 (thermal binds). With scales 0.10 m and 10 K the margins are 0.2 and 0.3 in either system | Unit-normalized predicates (§24.5; C-30); the unit-scaling metamorphic relation of §38.7 | §24.5 |
| CX-260 | Multiply stage pass rates to get the mission rate | Twenty equally likely worlds, two stages each passing in 19: 18 of 20 if the failures differ, 19 of 20 if they coincide; 0.95² = 0.9025 is neither | Stage pass rates are reported jointly (§24.5) | §24.5 |
| CX-261 | Report energy per completed trial only | Design A with one 10 J success and one 20 J failure against design B with two 12 J successes: 10 J against 12 J conditional on completion, 30 J against 12 J per completion; the two figures order the designs oppositely | Both energy figures are published (§24.8) | §24.8 |
| CX-262 | Integrate the current, then the wheel speed, explicitly | The wheel-loop row has spectral radius 0.9207 with the specified feedforward R·i; at a 0.5 ms step explicit Euler that updates the current before the wheel speed gives 1.497 (and 1.854 for the R·i_ref case of 1.4820), turning the stable case unstable | Actuator stage integration; GATE-ARENA-04 | §24.10 |
| CX-263 | A static screen can settle the sill question | In the creep limit design A docks in about one condition in ninety and B in fewer; in a planar pilot of 156 rows, 62 passed the dynamic scorer and failed the quasi-static screen, and none passed the static screen alone | A static rejection is a candidate for a dynamic test; momentum, impact and drive transients are in scope | §24.10 |
| CX-264 | A comparison may leave rotor inertia and controller rate unpinned | In an exploratory planar sweep the two designs' success fractions were 0.093 and 0.780 at 100 Hz with zero rotor inertia and 1.000 and 1.000 at 1 kHz or with added inertia: the comparison measured loop stability | Mechanism controls of fixture 1; pinned controller arm (§24.12) | §24.10 |
| CX-265 | A fixed-n study can return any of its verdicts | At n = 64 the rule of fixture 1 has 521, 521, 0 and 1,103 outcomes: no outcome is a practical tie, since perfect concordance gives [−0.0662, +0.0662]; a tie is first reachable at n = 86, for (0, 0) only | Reachability pre-analysis (`eval.preanalyze`); FROB-REF-532 | §24.10, §24.12 |
| CX-266 | Look at a fixed-n test after every pair and stop when it rejects | An exact one-sided sign test at nominal 5% on null pairs: 13/256 = 5.08% after 8 looks, 8.76% after 16, 12.9% after 32, 17.4% after 64 | Study types are never mixed (INV-ARENA-05); FROB-REF-552 | §24.12 |
| CX-267 | Resubmit candidates to the same held-out scenes until one passes | Independent attempts at 5% each: fourteen null submissions give a 51% chance of at least one false promotion | Level α_i = α/[i·(i + 1)] on fresh sealed clusters (INV-QUAL-02, -03); GATE-QUAL-01 | §24.13 |
| CX-268 | More contact area means more traction | Limiting model on a split-grip boundary: at z = +12 mm the 40 mm sole has capacity 0.690 against 0.619 for the 70 mm sole; at z = −12 mm it has 0.360 against 0.431. The ranking reverses with the sign of the offset | Fixture 2 decision rule; fidelity-dependent rankings are reported as such | §24.11 ≡ CX-179 |
| CX-269 | Concatenate separately recorded stage episodes to show a mission | The end state of one stage need not lie in the entry set of the next (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Missions are single episodes with until-clauses (§24.5) | §24.5 |

### N.22 §25 Augur: mission queries and inverse design

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-270 | The midpoint of two feasible designs is feasible | Feet at (±1 − e, ±1) m, each capped at 0.5 N, load W at the origin; the positive-x pair carries W·(1 + e)/2. (e, W) = (0, 1.9) and (0.9, 1) each need 0.95 N and pass; the midpoint (0.45, 1.45) needs 841/800 N from a pair capped at 1 N | Convexity claimed only in a chart where the constraints are affine with design-independent mission vertices (§25.13) | §25.4.7 ≡ CX-010 |
| CX-271 | Variants that are pairwise compatible can share a controller | Commands (x, y) ∈ [0, 1]²; three variants need x ≥ 2/3, y ≥ 2/3 and x + y ≤ 1. Every pair has a common command; the triple needs x + y ≥ 4/3 and x + y ≤ 1 | Minimal conflict sets, not pairs (§25.18) | §25.4.7, §25.18 |
| CX-272 | A command exists for each design, so one exists for the family | Pusher y = max(0, g·u − d), g ∈ [1/2, 2], d ∈ [0, 1/4], target [3/4, 5/4]: the per-build command set is all of [1/2, 2]; the shared command set is empty | The quantifier prefix is explicit in the query and in the certificate (§25.4.2); FROB-REF-826 | §25.4.2, §25.4.7 |
| CX-273 | A feasible allocation exists, so the body is admitted | Stance at half-width 1/4 m: the active allocation (155, 135, 105, 5) N fits 160 N ratings; an equal-spring passive suspension produces (175, 115, 85, 25) N | The authority that realizes the allocation, and the observations that arrive in time, are part of the certificate | §25.4.7 |
| CX-274 | The support polygon contains the load and total capacity suffices | Of 2,304 bodies in the stance grid, this screen accepts 1,596 that the exact model rejects | Per-foot ratings and moment balance together | §25.4.7, §25.22 |
| CX-275 | Sample points in a cell fail, so the cell is excluded | Any cell that straddles a boundary: the 9 boundary cells of the 561-cell docking grid | A certificate valid over the cell; otherwise the cell is unresolved | §25.4.7 |
| CX-276 | Both stages succeed 95% of the time, so the mission succeeds 90.25% | Twenty worlds, each stage failing in one: 18/20 if the failures differ, 19/20 if they coincide | Joint law or additive budgets (§25.3.5) | §25.4.7 |
| CX-277 | Each projection of a product covers its marginal outcomes, so the dominance check may enclose them separately | Paired outcomes (0, 0) and (1, 1) project onto {0, 1} for both robots and hide the unreachable (1, 0) | Enclose the joint outcome set (§25.16; C-40) | §25.4.7, §25.16 |
| CX-278 | The per-component limits are met, so the assembly is feasible; and its converse, an additive peak rule | Two fingers each need one powered tick and the bus powers one per tick: zero one-tick schedules, two two-tick schedules. Local motor limits are identical in both cases, and an additive peak rule prunes the feasible two-tick case | The joint, time-dependent resource contract (§25.17) | §25.4.7 |
| CX-279 | The probe separates every admitted mechanism, so its verdict is safe | The three-law probe scores 675 of 675 in class; a fourth mechanism shares the probe's early trace and reverses all 225 held-out decisions | Conditional witness; direct measurement of the target is always admitted (§25.14) | §25.4.7, §25.14 |
| CX-280 | A root of the synthesis equations is a mechanism | Crank-rocker 1, 4, 3 on ground 4: B = (11/5, ±12/5) at crank angle π lie on two assembly circuits | Circuit and branch certificates (§25.21.3) | §25.4.7, §25.21.3 |
| CX-281 | A Lie-bracket direction is reachable | ẋ = u with u ∈ [0, 1]: rank one, and negative x is unreachable from zero | The integrated finite witness | §25.4.7 |
| CX-282 | A certificate that held for the declared worlds holds when a world is added | Docking with gains {1, 2} admits ε < (U + 1/2)/4; adding gain 33/32 at ε = 1/64 leaves no exact docking, best worst-case error 1/65 m, and 34,590 positive labels on the grid reverse | Certificates are bound to the declared hypothesis support; revalidation polarity (§25.8) | §25.22 |
| CX-283 | A static rejection is a rejection | Of 156 sill cases, 94 agree between sampled statics and a planar dynamic scorer and 62 static rejections succeed dynamically | A static rejection is a candidate for a dynamic test, not a verdict | §25.22 |
| CX-284 | Compare strict predicates in unguarded single precision | On a 65,536-row docking-boundary screen: 452 false admissions and 23,438 false exclusions on the near grid; with the guard and exact fallback, zero final errors and identical bytes on every placement | Exact-integer kernel class and guarded `f32` (§35) | §25.12, §25.22 |
| CX-285 | Run the speed test, then interpret it | Pusher: composed over specialized median time 1.345 warm; shared checking and serialization are 94% of the specialized batch, so a zero-cost solver reaches about 1.46. The test could not have shown a large ratio | Compute the ceiling before any speed test (§38.4) | §25.22 |
| CX-286 | Repeated reads of a sensor always add information | Majority vote at 3/4 gives 27/32, 459/512, 3807/4096 for 3, 5, 7 independent reads, and stays at 3/4 when the reads share one fault bit | Error dependence is declared (§25.10) | §25.10, §25.22 ≡ CX-052 |
| CX-287 | A memo keyed by a semantic receipt beats a dependency graph, or may omit an input | Four edit states: both do 3 pair evaluations and 2 reductions and match fresh replay; omitting one dependency inherits a stale promotion | Complete memo keys; no reuse claim over a correctly keyed graph (§28) | §25.22 |

### N.23 §26 Nemesis: adversarial worlds and rare failures

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-288 | A world reduced by delta debugging is the smallest failing world | The world fails when {a, b} or {c, d, e} is present. With edits listed (a, c, d, e, b), the halves {a, c} and {d, e, b} pass, the singletons and {e, b} pass, the complement {c, d, e, b} fails, and the result is {c, d, e}: 1-minimal with three edits, while {a, b} exists | 1-minimal is the only claim; the one- and two-edit subset check (78 tests at most for 12 edits) labels a world minimum among pairs | §26.7 |
| CX-289 | Any world that defeats the design is a counterexample | A doorway narrower than the body defeats every controller; an oracle that checks a point robot calls it solvable | ADR-NEMESIS-02, INV-NEMESIS-01: only worlds with a `solvable-causal` or `plant-feasible` witness count | §26.5 |
| CX-290 | A good sampler discounts the price of proving absence | At δ = 0.05 a bound of 10⁻⁴ needs 29,956 nominal failure-free episodes, 10⁻⁵ needs 299,572 and 10⁻⁶ needs 2,995,731, whatever the explorer does; inside the qualification lane the tenth request needs 76,963 for 10⁻⁴ | Two lanes: exploration finds, nominal or bounded-weight sampling certifies (§26.9) | §26.9 |
| CX-291 | Importance weights may be unbounded if the proposal is good | A mixture without its nominal share on a mis-centred proposal misses the Gaussian tail P(z > 4) = 3.167 × 10⁻⁵; with q = ε·p + (1 − ε)·p_shifted every weight is at most 1/ε = 10 | Defensive mixture with bounded weights (§26.9) | §26.9 |
| CX-292 | Forks of a deterministic simulator give independent children | A walk with no noise after the fork returns identical children; the splitting estimate is refused | Fresh randomness after a fork (§26.9; C-27) | §26.9, §26.14 |
| CX-293 | A comparison among survivors validates the screen | An e-value between the eighth and ninth finalists is silent about a design the screen discarded at the first rung; no top-k design among 30 audited discards only bounds the false-discard rate at 9.5% | Audit of discarded strata and planted winners (§26.10); label `screen-biased` | §26.10 |
| CX-294 | A battery made of past failures is a good battery | It overfits them; the hard-world set is refused as the only content of a battery, as a confirmation sample (FROB-REF-567) and as nominal draws for a bound (FROB-REF-560) | §26.8; one slot in five of a covering battery is drawn from held-out scenes (§24.9) | §26.8 |
| CX-295 | A failure found under one contact law is a failure of the design | Planted box-cone traction and energy-pumping penetration recovery vanish under a law variant and are typed `artifact-candidate`; a genuine planted failure survives every variant | The artifact guard (§26.6) | §26.6 |

### N.24 §27 Autopsy: counterfactual debugging

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-296 | A Shapley summary tells joint causes from alternative causes | Two factors that rescue only together (v of the pair 1, of each single 0) and two that each rescue alone (v = 1 for either single and the pair) both have Shapley values (½, ½); the repair sets are {1, 2} and {1} or {2} | The coalition table is the result; the summary is a summary (§27.7) | §27.7 |
| CX-297 | A positive Shapley value shows a factor is necessary, and values are nonnegative | v(∅) = 0, v({1}) = 1, v({2}) = 0, v({1, 2}) = 0 gives (½, −½): a single-factor repair exists and factor 2 is negative because applying it cancels the rescue | §27.7; labels `but-for`, `joint`, `alternative` come from the table | §27.7 |
| CX-298 | Apply a design edit at a mid-episode fork to see whether it prevents the failure | Thermal node, time constant 60 s, ambient 25 °C, trip at 115 °C. Ratio 12 trips at 60·ln(192/102) = 37.95 s. Ratio 16 from the start peaks at 82.0 °C and passes; ratio 16 applied at 36.95 s finds the winding at 113.3 °C and trips at 42.4 s | Design parameters re-run from the episode start (§27.8); GATE-AUTOPSY-06 | §27.8 |
| CX-299 | Bisect for the first tick at which two traces differ | Two traces of eight ticks differ at ticks 1 and 5: a midpoint search on the pointwise predicate probes tick 3 or 4, sees agreement and reports tick 5; the chained digest reports tick 1 | Bisection only on a monotone, decided predicate (§27.10); the same rule for `design.bisect` over a lineage | §27.10 |
| CX-300 | Any flip under an intervention is attribution | Doubling map with failure on the fortieth iterate: a 10⁻¹² placebo is amplified by 2⁴⁰ ≈ 1.1 × 10¹² and about half of placebo forks flip | Placebo forks and the label `chaotic: unattributable` (§27.5) | §27.5 |
| CX-301 | A contrast between forks is a proof of cause | A contrast is a replayable experiment of class `Tested` on its tier (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | §27.13 | §27.13 |

### N.25 §28 Atelier: the studio loop, analyses, comparison, lineage, cascade, decision cores

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-302 | Support polygon plus total actuator capacity is a stance screen (A-08) | In an exhaustive stance enumeration of 11,520 body and load cases the screen accepts 1,596 bodies that the exact classifier rejects | Per-contact force limits enter the cone program; the result names its load-sharing model | §28.4 |
| CX-303 | Add peak currents on a shared bus (A-23) | Two fingers each need one powered tick and the bus powers one per tick: no joint schedule at a one-tick deadline, exactly two at two ticks. A rule that adds both peaks rejects the feasible two-tick design | The joint time-indexed contract; a prune is rejected if any discarded design is feasible under the same mission (§28.12) | §28.4 |
| CX-304 | Connection curvature tests for a misaligned mecanum roller (A-09) | A base with misaligned rollers still has spanning curvature, as a differential drive does; the defect is a rank drop in the connection, reported by the growth vector of A-07 | A-07 and A-09 are separate analyses | §28.4 |
| CX-305 | The mechanical symmetry group is the policy's symmetry (A-03) | A quadruped with one forward lidar has a four-element group mechanically and a two-element group in closed loop | Only the observation-preserving subgroup is used for tying, augmentation and deduplication | §28.4 |
| CX-306 | A quasi-static energy exclusion needs no approach energy (A-20) | 8 kg on a 12 mm rise needs 0.94176 J; a 0.90 J budget excludes the passive crossing; 0.05 J of approach kinetic energy removes the exclusion | The budget includes kinetic energy at the start of the segment; the result is `outer` only for the enclosed model | §28.4 ≡ CX-076 |
| CX-307 | Per-reading accuracy is the value of sensor redundancy (A-17) | Seven independent readings at 0.75 give 3807/4096 ≈ 0.929; seven with a perfectly shared error stay at 0.75 | The alias analysis reads the declared error dependence of the cards | §28.4 ≡ CX-052 |
| CX-308 | An instantaneous-current model of the drive loop is adequate (A-22) | 75 mm wheel, total reflected inertia 0.00107890625 kg·m², target held for 10 ms: spectral radius 1.4820 with target-current feedforward and 0.9207 with measured-current feedforward; the instantaneous model gives 2.7075 for both | The hold is modelled exactly over the control period | §28.4 ≡ CX-262 |
| CX-309 | Identify friction from a convenient run (A-25) | F = min(k_t·I/r, μ·N), k_t = 0.2 N·m/A, r = 0.06 m: runs at 1 A and 2 A under 30 N and 40 N give 3.33 N and 6.67 N for μ = 0.4 and for μ = 0.8; at 3 A under 10 N they give 4 N and 8 N | Zero sensitivity is reported and an excitation in the traction-limited regime is requested | §28.4 |
| CX-310 | A late observation with the right content serves the decision (A-21) | A packet arriving at tick 1 serves decisions committing at ticks 2 and 4; delayed to tick 3 it serves the second and no longer the first | Arrival instants are compared with decision instants per decision, in rational time | §28.4 |
| CX-311 | Key paired seeds by (world, epoch) | On a 64-wide batch such seeds pair only morphologies whose indices agree modulo 64 | Pair by (world, replicate); a counter that depends on training progress is not part of an address (§28.6) | §28.6 |
| CX-312 | Pair exogenous draws by event order | Two tires on different paths need not receive equal friction samples at corresponding ticks; pairing "the k-th friction draw" pairs nothing (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Exogenous fields are indexed by physical coordinate (§28.6) | §28.6 |
| CX-313 | Binary search finds the first edit that changed the outcome | Outcomes along six versions are pass, fail, pass, pass, pass, fail: the midpoint passes, the search goes right and reports the last edit, while the first edit changed the outcome and the second changed it back | `design.bisect` returns an `a-change`; `first-change` only after the prefix is scanned or monotonicity is declared | §28.7 |
| CX-314 | Enlarging the hypothesis support slightly leaves inclusions valid | Docking with gains {1, 2}: adding gain 33/32, indistinguishable from gain 1 at an error radius of 1/64, makes every one of the 34,590 positive decisions on each test grid wrong | Revalidation polarity: "hypothesis support enlarged: recheck inclusions" (§28.13) | §28.13 ≡ CX-282 |
| CX-315 | A memo key may omit delivery time | Four-state camera fixture: with the complete key the evaluations are 2, 0, 0, 1 and the recomputations 1, 0, 0, 1; the key without delivery time keeps a stale promotion when delivery is delayed to tick 3 | Complete memo keys (§28.13, §28.14) | §28.13 |
| CX-316 | An optimum survives any change that keeps it feasible | Widening the doorway keeps the cost-9 cart feasible and admits a cheaper rival: feasibility is kept, optimality is revoked | Revalidation treats feasibility and optimality separately (§28.13) | §28.13 |
| CX-317 | An in-class-perfect probe is safe to act on | A probe synthesized for three admitted material laws is right in 675 of 675 in-class cases; a fourth, delayed law follows the same early trace and reverses all 225 held-out decisions; direct measurement at mission dwell is right in 900 of 900 | Measuring the target directly is always on the list (§28.16) | §28.16 |
| CX-318 | Rank agreement near the top can be assumed | Published neighbours report rank correlations of 0.40 overall and 0.50, 0.60, 0.83 on three robots over 17 designs, and sparse Kendall τ of 0.236, 0.740 and 0.159: agreement of 0.7 or better is not supported | The rank-agreement ledger; an unmeasured stratum is `discard-only` (§28.10) | §28.10 |

|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-319 | A mergeable quantile sketch is fine on the evidence path | A compactor of capacity two that keeps the lower of each sorted pair summarizes 1, 2, 3 as {1 with weight 2, 3} and 3, 2, 1 as {2 with weight 2, 1}: medians 1 and 2. Integer bin counts are 1, 1, 1 in both orders | Deterministic integer histograms (§29.7) | §29.7 |
| CX-320 | Bisect on the pointwise predicate "the states differ at tick t" | Two traces differ at ticks 1 and 5 and agree elsewhere: the midpoint search reports tick 5; the chained digest reports tick 1 | Never bisect on an undecided or pointwise predicate (§29.8) | §29.8 |
| CX-321 | Graft a warm start across a design edit in a fork | The 60 mm wheel's warm start grafted onto the 75 mm wheel is refused (FROB-REF-582) | Fork rules of §29.8; design parameters re-run from the start (§27.8) | §29.8, §29.19 |
| CX-322 | Downsample an impulse channel by averaging | An impulse train averaged over a window loses the momentum transfer that the sum conserves (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Channel kinds declare their downsampling rule (§29.5) | §29.5, §29.19 |

### N.27 §30 Ground: calibration against reality

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-326 | Fit the clock offset to the event being scored | The prediction says the wheel crosses the sill at 4.212 s and the log shows 4.180 s. The offset that aligns them reports a crossing-time error of zero and an offset of 32 ms: the whole error has moved into the clock | Clock fitting rule 2 (§30.7); GATE-GROUND-02 | §30.7 |
| CX-327 | Cross-correlating command and response fits the clock | A delay of 8 ms with an offset of −8 ms produces the same logs as no delay and no offset; without a shared hardware mark only "delay plus offset" is identified and the card's delay is not updated | Clock fitting rule 3 (§30.7); structural alias 5 | §30.7 |
| CX-328 | A good local fit identifies its parameters | Eight structural aliases: armature N²·J_r with link inertia (only I_link + N²·J_r appears when a joint moves alone); Coulomb friction with a constant bias in one direction; viscous friction with back-EMF damping b + k_t·k_e/R under voltage drive; torque constant with the scale of inertia and friction; delay with clock offset; friction coefficient with the torque limit; static friction when the run never dwells at zero velocity; contact stiffness with damping from one drop height | Identifiability checks before any fit (§30.8); GATE-GROUND-03 | §30.8 |
| CX-329 | Friction can be identified from any traction run | F = min(k_t·I/r, μ·N), k_t = 0.2 N·m/A, r = 0.06 m: at 1 A and 2 A under 30 N and 40 N the drawbar force is 3.33 N and 6.67 N for μ = 0.4 and for μ = 0.8; at 3 A under 10 N it is 4 N and 8 N | Excitation design: the traction-limited regime must be excited (§30.8) | §30.8 |
| CX-330 | Pool calibration data across regimes to get more points | Pooled conformal calibration at a 90% target covers 20 of 20 episodes in one regime and 16 of 20 in a shifted one: 36 of 40, nominal overall and 80% where it matters | The cell is the bucket (§30.12) | §30.12 |
| CX-331 | Score a fit on its own calibration data | A fit class without the truth's Stribeck term passes on its calibration data and is rejected on sealed data | Frozen predictions and sealed trials (§30.11) | §30.21 |
| CX-332 | Matching the observed speed validates the model | Speed matched by a compensating error in k_t and friction; the energy cross-check marks `wrong-reason` | Energy cross-check in identification (§30.9) | §30.21 |
| CX-333 | Twenty clean real trials support "under 5%" | Zero failures in 20 trials give an upper bound of 13.9% | Reachability pre-analysis at registration (§30.11) | §30.21 |
| CX-334 | Recovering planted parameters from a simulator is a Reality result | The truth simulator is the referee with planted parameters; everything it emits is `origin = "synthetic"` and the claim stays `no-claim` | INV-GROUND-06: `synthetic` is never promoted | §30.21 |
| CX-335 | Agreement between tiers shows the model is right | Every tier consumes the same actuator card, so Fidelity and Parity results say nothing about whether the card describes a real motor | §30.2; the report line giving the share of `generic` and `assumed` card fields | §30.2 |

### N.28 §31 Foundry: structure, manufacture, lifecycle

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-336 | Assemble the peak of every load channel into one design case | A simply supported span L carries P at L/3 at one instant and at 2L/3 at another: the mid-span moment is P·L/6 each time; the assembled case gives P·L/3, a twofold over-design. With the second load of opposite sign the assembled case gives zero while each real case gives ±P·L/6: unsafe | Time-coherent cases only (ADR-FOUNDRY-03) | §31.6 |
| CX-337 | Symmetric limbs carry equal loads | A payload offset sideways breaks equal loading (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Limb histories are transformed to the canonical limb frame and unioned (§31.6) | §31.6 |
| CX-338 | Count rainflow cycles per episode and add the results | Sequence −2, 1, −3, 5, −1, 3, −4, 4, −2 in units of 40 MPa: ranges 3, 4, 6, 8, 9 with counts 0.5, 1.5, 0.5, 1.0, 0.5 and damage 8.752 × 10⁻⁶. Splitting after the fifth point and finishing each window loses the range-9 half cycle: 7.816 × 10⁻⁶, 10.7% low | Residues are carried between episodes and `finish` is called once (§31.8) | §31.8, §31.18 |
| CX-339 | Feed unsigned von Mises stress to a cycle counter | Unsigned stress changes the cycle count and is refused (no numeric case is given in the chapter) | Signed stress only (§31.8) | §31.8 |
| CX-340 | A zero first-order sensitivity means the feature may be loosened | q(x) = x² at nominal zero: the sensitivity is zero and a first-order allocator loosens without limit, while the deviation at tolerance t is t² | FROB-REF-165; the band-extreme check (§31.11) | §31.11 |
| CX-341 | Prune a design whose component peaks add past the bus limit | Two gripper fingers each need one powered tick and the bus powers one per tick: no schedule at a one-tick deadline, exactly two at two ticks, with identical motor limits. Adding both peaks prunes a feasible design | A prune is rejected if any discarded design is independently feasible under the same mission (§31.12) | §31.12 |
| CX-342 | Q15 multiplication cannot overflow | (−1)·(−1) is 0x8000·0x8000 = 0x40000000, which shifts to 0x8000 and reads as −1; the negation of −32768 overflows | Fixed-point obligations (§31.13); FROB-REF-827 | §31.13 |
| CX-343 | An integrator without a clamp is safe if each step is small | Adding 400 counts per tick from zero overflows sixteen bits at tick 82; without a clamp the one-step query returns the state 32,400 at once | Fixed-point obligations (§31.13) | §31.13 |
| CX-344 | Evaluate a life law at the average condition | Insulation life halving per 10 K, half the time at T_ref and half at T_ref + 20 K: life is consumed at 2.5/τ_ref; the law at the mean temperature gives 2.0/τ_ref and overstates service duration by 25% | Micro windows average the rate, never the input (§31.14) | §31.14 |

### N.29 §32 Tether: CLI, daemon, MCP, dashboard, viewer

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-345 | Check idempotency by looking up the key, then inserting, in two statements | Requests A and B carry the same key and payload. A looks up and misses; B looks up and misses; A inserts; B inserts. Two jobs run and the budget is charged twice | One transaction with a unique index on (principal, verb, key) binds key, digest, reservation and job (§32.7) | §32.7 |
| CX-346 | Paginate by offset | Rows at t = 1, 2, 3, 4, page size 2. Page one returns {1, 2}. A chunk from a slower world commits a row at t = 1.5. Offset 2 now returns {2, 3}: row 2 is repeated | Pagination pinned to a committed frontier (§32.8.1); FROB-REF-806, -807 | §32.8.1 |
| CX-347 | Use a cursor on time alone | Two worlds each have a sample at t = 2 and the page ends on the first; "after t = 2" skips the second | Total-order key (instant, world address, channel, sample index) (§32.8.1) | §32.8.1 |
| CX-348 | Send 64-bit integers as JSON numbers | 2⁵³ + 1 = 9,007,199,254,740,993 decodes to 9,007,199,254,740,992 in a client that reads doubles | `int53` and `u64str` / `i64str` schema types; FROB-REF-808 (§32.8.2) | §32.8.2 |
| CX-349 | Key telemetry chunks by tick range | A retry splits a shard, and ranges [0, 100) and [50, 150) both cover ticks 50 to 99; with range keys both commit | Fixed logical chunk partition per (world, episode); FROB-REF-810 (§32.10) | §32.10 |
| CX-350 | Key telemetry by (world, tick) | A reset reuses tick zero, and the second episode overwrites the first | The episode index is part of the key (§32.10) | §32.10 |
| CX-351 | Priority without preemption protects interactive jobs | One device, 90% of jobs 10 s and 10% of jobs 30 min (E[S] = 189 s, E[S²] = 324,090 s²), M/G/1 at utilization 0.7: a short job waits 2,001 s first-come-first-served, 621 s under non-preemptive priority and 0.17 s plus one quantum under preempt-resume. The wait under priority is the remainder of the long job already on the device | Slot dispatch with preemption at kernel-batch boundaries (§32.11.2; DC-09) | §32.11.2 |

### N.30 §33 Suite consumption

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-352 | A branch on abs(ω) ≤ 10⁻¹² rad/s separates straight from curved motion | θ = 32 rad, wheel speeds 1 and 1 + 6·10⁻¹³ m/s, track 0.5 m, Δt = 1/400 s: ω = 1.2·10⁻¹² rad/s takes the curved branch, ω·Δt = 3·10⁻¹⁵ rad is below half the spacing of doubles at 32 (2⁻⁴⁸ ≈ 3.55·10⁻¹⁵), θ′ rounds to θ and both differences are exactly zero. Over 400 steps the true displacement is one metre, about (0.83422336, 0.55142668) m | The half-angle chord form Δp = v·Δt·sinc(δ/2)·(cos(θ + δ/2), sin(θ + δ/2)); INT-FSIM-05 | §33.11 row 2 |
| CX-353 | A stride tuple means the same thing in every array library | A contiguous 2×2 single-precision view has strides (8, 4) in bytes and (2, 1) in elements; (8, 4) copied into an element-stride view over a 16-element buffer addresses element 12 at [1, 1], where element 3 belongs, in bounds. Byte origin 4 with strides (8, −4) addresses (1, 0, 3, 2), which unsigned strides cannot express | A typed converter at the export boundary; no bare stride tuple crosses it; INT-FNP-02 | §33.11 row 3 |
| CX-354 | A dimension vector is all the typing a quantity needs | A torque and an energy have the same vector (kilogram 1, metre 2, second −2); dimension checking alone cannot separate them | Distinct aliases over one dimension; the compile-fail test adds a torque to an energy (§33.12) | §33.12 |
| CX-355 | Import the mesh and trust it | A corridor whose far half was never observed appears as empty space in a naive import, and a traversal certificate over it would be false; with the coverage mask the same query returns unknown | Unscanned space is unknown, never free (§33.15; §19) | §33.15 |
| CX-356 | A fallback that exists on paper is a fallback | The worker-process fallback is exercised at M0 although inactive: a 2,000-step rollout through the worker must return bit-equal chunk digests, also after the worker is killed mid-job | INT-FSIM-18 | §33.14 |
| CX-357 | A sibling's training code can serve as an oracle or a baseline | The suite's only PPO update reverses the clip branch for negative advantage (finite differences −0.25, −0.5, 0 where 0, −0.5, −1 are correct), forms the critic gradient from the stored value (0 where 2 is correct) and forwards two reset episodes as one sequence (ratio 1.582459 where 1 is correct) | Not linked, not an oracle, not a baseline; INT-FSIM-06; the same three probes test this project's PPO | §33.11 row 1 ≡ CX-252 |

### N.31 §34 Workspace, crates, formats

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-358 | Hash the file as written | Two files that differ in key order or whitespace, such as `{"b":1,"a":2}` and `{"a":2,"b":1}`, would be two designs, and every memoized analysis of the first would miss on the second | Canonical serialization before hashing (§34.9); GATE-WS-02 | §34.9 |
| CX-359 | Hash the in-memory value | A hash of a debug rendering or of a struct's layout changes with the compiler, so the same design would change identity at a pin move and every cached result would be orphaned | Identity is the hash of the canonical bytes (§34.9) | §34.9 |
| CX-360 | The view is lossless, so hash either form | The S-expression and the JSON of one design are different bytes; a cache keyed on whichever arrived first holds two entries for one design and two verdicts a reader could cite as independent | Only the canonical JSON is hashed (§34.9) | §34.9 |
| CX-361 | Tolerate unknown fields | A reader that ignores an unknown required field such as a contact-law selector simulates a different robot from the one described and reports it under the same identity | Unknown required fields are refused (§34.9) | §34.9 |
| CX-362 | Reuse `frob-core`'s hashing in the checker | A canonicalization defect in `frob-core` that drops a field before hashing would be reproduced by the checker, and a bundle whose statement differs from the one the proposer evaluated would pass; with two implementations the hashes disagree | INV-LEMMA-05: `frob-cert` depends on the standard library only; GATE-WS-03 | §34.11 |
| CX-363 | Crates inside one layer need no order | `frob-arena` could depend on `frob-opt` for comparison while `frob-opt` depends on `frob-arena` for batteries; Cargo rejects the cycle only after both are written | Rule L-1's intra-layer rank; `check-layers`; GATE-WS-01 | §34.3 |

### N.32 §35 Numerics and determinism

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-364 | A state tolerance implies agreement on the verdict | A success predicate x ≥ 1 m, one placement ending at 1.0000001 m and the other at 0.9999999 m: the state error is 2·10⁻⁷ m, inside any envelope, and the verdicts differ. Conversely two placements may end a metre apart and agree that the robot fell | ADR-NUM-03: under class D3 task decisions are tested beside states (§35.4); GATE-SPARK-16 | §35.4 |
| CX-365 | Zero discordant decisions on a small ensemble shows decision equivalence | With zero discordant worlds the one-sided 95% upper bound on the discordant rate is about 3/n, so fewer than 300 worlds cannot show 1% at all | Decision-equivalence thresholds on a frozen ensemble of at least 4,096 worlds (GATE-SPARK-16) | §35.4 |
| CX-366 | Seeding the generator makes two arms a replayable pair | Seeding fixes the draws, not the order of a reduction. A difference of one unit in the last place grows like e^(λt) and reaches order one after about ln(2²⁴)/λ ≈ 16.6/λ seconds; if uncontrolled arithmetic lowers the pair correlation from 0.95 to 0.50, a comparison needs (1 − 0.50)/(1 − 0.95) = 10 times the episodes (illustrative values) | Minimum classes of §35.3: paired statistics refused with an arm of class D4 (`FROB-REF-561`), attribution refused below D2 (`FROB-REF-562`); GATE-SPARK-17 | §35.3 |
| CX-367 | Padding lanes with +0.0 is neutral for a sum | The sum of the single active element −0.0 is −0.0 alone and +0.0 when one +0.0 lane is appended, because (−0.0) + (+0.0) = +0.0; the values compare equal, the bits differ, and a digest over bits changes with capacity | INV-NUM-02: padding holds the neutral element of its consumer (−0.0 for sums, 1.0 for products, ±∞ for minimum and maximum); GATE-SPARK-15 | §35.5 |
| CX-368 | A batch-wide observation normalizer is harmless | Two worlds observing 1 and 3 and normalized by their batch mean see −1 and +1; the first world alone sees 0, takes a different action and follows a different trajectory | INV-NUM-03: statistics across worlds never feed state; normalizers are frozen within a collection segment; GATE-SPARK-15 | §35.5 |
| CX-369 | Anchored offsets can be kept in [0, 8) | A body that drifts to −2⁻²⁵ m must add 8. The number 8 − 2⁻²⁵ is not an `f32` (the spacing just below 8 is 2⁻²¹), so the sum rounds to 8.0: the position has moved and has left the cell's range | ADR-NUM-06: offsets centred in [−4, 4); PO-NUM-01 (re-anchoring exact for magnitudes 4 to 16); `FROB-REF-249` | §35.8 |
| CX-370 | Absolute positions can be held in single precision | Single precision resolves about 7.6 µm at 100 m from the origin and worse beyond, too coarse for contact; a cell-relative offset in [−4, 4) m resolves at worst 2⁻²² m, about 0.24 µm | Anchored positions: an integer cell index plus an `f32` offset (§35.8); GATE-SPARK-05 at distances up to 1 km | §35.8 |
| CX-371 | Equal seeds give two designs the same disturbances | A sequential stream r₀, r₁, r₂. Design A draws a push (r₀) and a friction (r₁). Design B has one more leg and draws a per-leg value first, so its push is r₁ and its friction r₂. The seeds are equal and the two designs were pushed differently | Counter-based streams keyed by address and by the physical entity perturbed (§35.9); INV-NUM-04 | §35.9 |
| CX-372 | Scale the matrix to make the Cholesky solve accurate | Cholesky without pivoting is invariant under two-sided scaling by powers of two: the computed factor of D·H·D is exactly D times the computed factor of H, so scaling does not change one bit of the solve. What scaling changes is the meaning of tolerances and regularization around it | ADR-NUM-04; PO-NUM-02 (bitwise metamorphic test); the conditioning figure κ̂ is reported, not improved (§35.10) | §35.10 |
| CX-373 | One absolute residual tolerance means the same for every row | H = diag(1, 10⁻⁸) and a residual (0, 10⁻⁷): its norm passes an absolute tolerance of 10⁻⁶ while the error in the second unknown is 10⁻⁷/10⁻⁸ = 10. In scaled variables with D = diag(1, 10⁴) the residual is (0, 10⁻³) and the test fails, as it should | Tolerances and regularization are stated in scaled variables (§35.10 item 2); GATE-SPARK-18 | §35.10 |

### N.33 §36 The performance program

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-374 | Env-steps and world-steps can be quoted side by side without a decimation | 400 k env-steps/s of training at d = 10 is 4.0 M world-steps/s inside training, and cannot stand beside a physics-only figure of 2.0 M world-steps/s on the same card. Incumbents do not share a decimation either: one publishes d = 5, another reports 1.20 M env-steps/s of physics as 4.8 M physics steps per second (d = 4) | ADR-PERF-02 and INV-PERF-01: two units, each written with its decimation, with the ordering checked on every report; GATE-SPARK-19 | §36.2 |
| CX-375 | A faster environment step delivers the ten-fold | On one incumbent stack the environment step is 87% of training wall-clock, so removing it entirely gives at most 1/(1 − 0.87) = 7.8; on another physics is about 29% of the environment step, so a faster physics kernel alone gives at most 1.4; removing inference and learning gives at most 1.15. On quadruped briefs no single phase reaches ten-fold | Engine throughput is a parity requirement (§36.1); ADR-PERF-05, ceiling first; `FROB-REF-866` | §36.4 |
| CX-376 | A pruning ratio multiplies with a device speedup | 100 candidates of 10 s each; pruning removes 90; the ten survivors are the hard ones and accelerate by 2, not by the 5 measured on the full set. The result is 1,000/50 = 20, not 10 × 5 = 50 | Rule 2 of the speedup bound: a pruning ratio is never multiplied by a speedup measured on work already removed (§36.5) | §36.5 |
| CX-377 | Family batching gives a large speedup by itself | A generation of 256 designs at 16 worlds and 10 s each is about 10 s of simulation; a mixed batch removes switching and adds padding (30%). If a switch costs 1 s, f = 256/266 = 0.96 and the bound is 266/13 ≈ 20; if a switch costs 0.1 s, f = 0.72 and the bound is 35.6/13 ≈ 2.7. The gain depends almost entirely on the incumbent's cost of switching designs | speedup ≤ 1 / (1 − f + f/r + h), with f, r and h measured on the strong baseline's critical path; the switch cost is measured at GATE-SPARK-21 before the experiment of §17 is funded | §36.5 |
| CX-378 | Optimize the component first and measure the gain afterwards | A specialized solver missed its speed gate at 1.35 warm and 1.09 cold; a later measurement showed that about 94% of the specialized batch was shared checking and serialization, so a solver of zero cost would have reached about 1.46. The ceiling was computable before the experiment ran | Ceiling first: an experiment whose ceiling is below its goal is refused (`FROB-REF-866`) | §36.5 |
| CX-379 | Two cards train twice as fast | An incumbent's own two-card run takes about 325 s against about 410 s on one card, a gain of 1.26: about 45 s of the 410 s is compilation, the learner runs on one stream, and sample efficiency falls as the batch grows | Weak and strong scaling are measured separately (rows 5 and 6 of §36.3); KILL-SHARD below a paired lower bound of 1.6 | §36.6, §36.3 |
| CX-380 | Gradients from shards can be combined as the mean of shard means | One sample with gradient 1 and three with gradient 0 give 0.25. Split as {1} and {0, 0, 0}, the mean of shard means gives (1 + 0)/2 = 0.5; with an empty shard it divides by zero or halves the answer | The distributed normalization rule: a sum of sums in device order divided by the global valid count (§36.13); GATE-SPARK-20 | §36.13, §35.7 |
| CX-381 | Device memory limits the number of worlds | By the byte model a world is about 35 kB (quadruped) or 97 kB (humanoid): 8,192 worlds are 0.28 GB and 0.79 GB, 1 to 3% of a 24 GB card. Memory limits distinct scenes and camera frames, not worlds: two 640 × 480 cameras on 8,192 worlds are 37.5 GiB | Memory arithmetic of §36.10 and §36.12; camera resolution and cadence are part of the task identity, not a performance knob | §36.10, §36.12 |
| CX-382 | A derived bandwidth ceiling can be used as a planning figure | With assumed traffic of 0.2 to 1.5 MB per humanoid world-step the estimate's central ceiling is 0.7 to 1.1 M world-steps/s, below the 2.55 M an incumbent measures on the same class of card: either a step touches fewer bytes than assumed or the efficiency is higher | Bytes touched per world-step is measured per launch and reported in the disclosure block (GATE-SPARK-22) | §36.11 |
| CX-383 | The referee can check every device world | A device produces T·h world-seconds per second: for the humanoid row 1.7 M × 2 ms = 3,400. Twenty-four cores at a real-time factor of 0.2 to 1.0 cover 4.8 to 24 of them, 0.14 to 0.7% | The referee is a sampler; its share is reported (row 17 of §36.3); `batch-cpu` shadows more (550 world-seconds per second, 16%) | §36.9 |

### N.34 §37 Safety, security, privacy

| CX | The tempting shortcut | The smallest case that breaks it | The rule it protects | Defined in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CX-384 | Let `eval.qualify` say which worlds failed | An agent could run `nemesis.search` near those worlds, patch for them and resubmit; the α_i schedule would then bound an error rate for a procedure that is no longer the one being run. The receipt carries only the decision, the index, the level spent, the units used and a hash | INV-QUAL-01; FROB-REF-730, -840; GATE-SAFE-03 | §37.5 |

### N.35 Seeding the negative-control registry

Counts at plan time (FACT, computed from the entries above): 384 entries; 17 state their refutation in words
only; 13 share a fixture with an earlier entry in another chapter and are marked "≡".

How an entry becomes a control (DESIGN; the record format is in §38.11):

| Kind of shortcut | Kind of control | Example |
|---|---|---|
| A wrong formula or implementation choice | `mutation`: a test build in which the shortcut replaces the rule | Explicit Euler that updates the current before the wheel speed (CX of §24.10); a pyramidal friction cone; a two-statement idempotency check |
| A wrong inference from a right computation | `planted-input`: a request that asks for the unsound conclusion and must be refused | A tie verdict at n = 64; a certificate offered with its quantifiers reordered; a scale-fitted estimator score |
| A wrong screen or prune | `planted-design`: a design the shortcut would accept or discard wrongly | A body the support-polygon screen accepts and the exact classifier rejects; the two-tick bus schedule that an additive peak rule prunes |
| A wrong assumption about worlds | `planted-world`: a world or world pair that separates the shortcut from the rule | The T-junction alias; the third docking gain 33/32; the doorway narrower than the body |
| A wrong experimental procedure | `a-a` or a scripted adversary | Peeking at a fixed-n test; resubmission to reused clusters; audit bits chosen after the outcomes |

The five fixtures that the thesis chapters require of this catalogue are present: the quantifier-order fixture, the
midpoint counterexample, the coupled-bus fixture, the pre-slip aliasing fixture and the omitted-law fixture. The
pre-slip aliasing fixture is stated without numbers in every chapter that names it; its numeric instance is owed by
the energy-budget tier (§22.11) before it can be registered.

Entries are registered against the gates of the rule they protect. An entry whose rule has no gate is a finding
about the plan, reported at the next milestone exit: either the rule gains a gate or the entry is withdrawn with a
recorded reason (PO-GAUNT-03).

**Inconsistencies found in the Phase 1 parts while compiling (for the lead)**

- The eleven items of M.38 (1.46 against 1.43; 7.8 against 7.69; 515 s against 512.5 s; film tile counts; 190 GB
  against 85% of 222 GiB; 2.6% against 2.5%; 76,963 against 76,959; a control that cannot fail; the memory share of
  §1.8; C-15's missing level; C-32's 0.64).
- Rank-agreement thresholds: the reconciliation fixes 0.7 and 0.5; the mode table of §28.10 switches at 0.5 and 0.2
  and calls 0.5 and 0.7 "the kill thresholds of the calibration experiment".
- Device budget: §19.15 admits to 85% of the working-set limit, §36.10 to 80%; both then use 190 GB.
- Directory entry size: 4 bytes per coarse cell in §19.4 and §21.20, 6 bytes in §19.15.
- §36.3 row 1: tolerance 0.67 of a 4.0 M planning value is 2.7 M, and the interim floor is 2.0 M.
- §36.4 uses an incumbent quadruped cycle of 360 to 420 s in one item and about 354 s in another; §2.2 and §2.3 use
  325 s and 300 s for two cards.
- Notation: §13 and §14 write 𝔾_mech and 𝔾_obs; §10 and §17 write Γ_mech and Γ_obs.
- Cross-references: §16.12 cites §17.12 where §17.13 is meant; §17.14 refers to nine template families that §14
  does not define (§14.10 has eighteen archetypes).
- §16.6 row capacities differ from the stated rule R = 4·C + 2·(actuated joints) for four classes.
- Backlash sign convention: θ/N − q in §15.6, q_out − q_m/N in §18.7.
- The P01 ledger lists provisional refusal codes 862 to 866 for Lemma; §12.7 and Appendix O use 826, 829, 833, 834
  and 835.
- Flags: §8.2 and §12.2 list three no-authorization flags for every artifact; build packets carry a fourth
  (`fabrication_released`).
- Tier names (`referee`, `batch-cpu`, `gpu-cuda`, `gpu-metal`) and step profiles (`lockstep`, `strict`, `referee`)
  share the word `referee`; Appendix M's tier column states which site is meant.
- Boundary corpus: §21.23 says 2 × 65,536 rows; §20.16 speaks of one 65,536-case grid.
- §34.6 fixes `xtask` at six checks that run no physics and record no gate; version 0.2's wording placed the
  negative-control registry "in `frob-conformance`", which this part keeps.
- Extraction note: chapters §0 to §21, §35 and §36 were extracted row by row from the body text as well as from
  their known-answer tables; chapters §22 to §34 and §37 were compiled from their known-answer tables, fixtures,
